**Layout**

Full-width white header with the title and subtitle both left-aligned on a common left edge at about 19% of slide width, with the body content below in a full-bleed light grey band from about 24.7% to 89% of slide height. Inside the band, five numbered columns start at about 3%, 23%, 43%, 63% and 83% of slide width, with a row of dotted arrows across the top and small labels above the arrows; the footer sits on white below the band with the page number at the left margin, a light grey wordmark logo at about 19% and the source line right-aligned near the right margin.

**Visuals**

Four dotted grey arrows run left to right across the grey band at about 34.6% of slide height, each a dotted horizontal line of dark grey #666666 with a small solid filled triangular arrowhead at its right end, forming a continuous progression from the 1958 label to the 5-10 years label. The arrows are segmented: arrow 1 spans about x 3.2 to 22.0, arrow 2 about x 22.9 to 42.0, arrow 3 about x 42.7 to 61.8, and arrow 4 about x 62.5 to 81.5. Five large numerals 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5 in a thin geometric sans-serif in orange-tan #CE7C3B mark levels 1 to 5 of vehicle autonomy, each sitting directly beneath the arrows at the column start. The 'Now' label sits above arrow 3 and the '5-10 years?' label above the end of arrow 4. Below the numerals are short descriptions of each level, one per column. A small light grey wordmark reading ANDREESSEN HOROWITZ in a high-contrast serif capital style sits in the footer; no photographs or charts are present.

**Design**

Background: Solid white #FFFFFF with a full-width light grey #F2F2F2 band from about 24.7% to 89% of slide height. Clean minimal corporate slide. Background is white #FFFFFF with a full-width grey band #F2F2F2 behind the body content. Title and subtitle are near-black #101010 and #000000 in a geometric humanist sans-serif (Proxima Nova Regular). Title text is about 5.9% of slide height, subtitle and body about 3.5%, labels about 3.5%, footer about 2.5%. Level numerals use Proxima Nova Thin about 17.3% of slide height in orange-tan #CE7C3B. Body and year labels are dark grey #3F3F3F; the word Level is light grey #A6A6A6; the source line is warm grey #7F7E7B; the page number is #666666. Arrows are dark grey dotted strokes with solid triangular heads. No shadows, gradients or rounded corners.

**Visuals**

Three outlined circles of equal size in a left-to-right row, with two solid grey right-pointing block arrows between them. Circle 1 (left): stroke dark grey #666666, about 0.3% of slide width thick, with bold heading 'Physical scale' and two body lines 'Electric changes / barriers to entry…' and 'But bashing metal is / still about scale'. Circle 2 (centre): stroke orange #C97A3A, same thickness, bold heading 'Data / network effects' and body lines 'Driving behaviour / data to feed ML', 'HD 3D maps', 'How many winners?'. Circle 3 (right): stroke pale blue-grey #C8D3E0, same thickness, bold heading 'On-demand / network effects' and body lines 'Virtuous circle: / density of cars + / density of riders' and 'Uber + Lyft'. Arrow 1 sits between circles 1 and 2 at about x 29.9%, y 54.9%, approximately 4.3% wide and 7.5% high. Arrow 2 sits between circles 2 and 3 at x 64.0%, y 54.0%, approximately 5.2% wide and 9.3% high. Both arrows are solid #A3A3A3 with no outline. No chart, photo or icon. The wordmark is a raster image of the text 'ANDREESSEN HOROWITZ' in a light grey serif capitals style.

Background: Solid white #FFFFFF, with a full-width #F2F2F2 light grey band from about 24.7% to 89% of slide height. ### Slide 73

**Text**

Self-driving car =
horseless carriage
First, we make the tech fit the old
patterns.
Then, we change to take advantage of
what’s new.
And removing the driver changes even
more than removing the horses.
72
ANDREESSEN HOROWITZ
Source: Daimler

**Layout**

Two-column split: a full-height light grey left panel (about 35% of slide width) holds the title, the body text and the footer page number and wordmark; the right area is white and holds one black-and-white photograph with a source line in the bottom-right corner. The title sits top-left at about 6.9% from the top with a left margin of about 3.1%. Body text is left-aligned, ragged right, starting at about 36.5% from the top. The page number sits bottom-left at about 92.8%; the wordmark sits to its right on the same baseline; the source line sits bottom-right, right-aligned to about 97% of the width.

**Visuals**

One photograph, a black-and-white historical image of a horseless carriage (early Daimler automobile). Subject: a boxy, closed-body, dark-bodied motor carriage with tall glazed side windows, a rear luggage box, a small plate reading 'Frei' on the front, a round dial on the side, and two brass-and-glass headlamps on the front right. It has four large spoked wheels with thin tyres; the front wheel is partly hidden behind the body. The car is in three-quarter view facing right, on a light, slightly snowy or dusty ground. Behind it are blurred pale buildings, bare trees, a white picket fence and a shrub. Lighting is soft, diffuse and even, with no hard shadows. Dominant colours are greys and black, with near-white highlights. The photo is not cropped on its subject, but its edges are feathered into white with a soft vignette, so it reads as floating rather than boxed. There is no overlay or tint. Also present: a faint grey Andreessen Horowitz wordmark in the footer, a light grey letterspaced logo, which is a logo element.

**Design**

Background: Split background: white #FFFFFF across the whole slide, with a flat light grey #F2F2F2 panel covering the left 35.1% of the width. Clean, minimal, document-style slide. The background is white, with a flat light grey (#F2F2F2) panel covering the left third with no border or shadow. Title text is near-black #101010 in ProximaNova-Regular, a geometric humanist sans-serif, regular weight, large (about 5.9% of slide height per line, with cap height about 4% of slide height). Body text is black #000000 in ProximaNova-Regular, regular weight, about 3.0% of slide height, with line spacing about 1.3 times the font size and paragraphs separated by one blank line. The page number is grey #666666 at about 2.5% of slide height. The source line is mid-grey #7F7E7B at about 2.5%. The wordmark is very pale grey, almost invisible against #F2F2F2. Accents: none. No shapes, lines, corner radii, gradients or shadows.

### Slide 77

**Text**

… And the biggest changes are unknowable
“Going to the opera in the year 2000”
76
ANDREESSEN HOROWITZ
Source: Albert Robida

**Layout**

Single-column layout with the title and subtitle left-aligned at x 19.1%, starting at y 6.9%, on a white header band. A full-bleed illustration fills the width of the slide from y 24.7% to the bottom edge. A semi-transparent white footer band across the bottom 10.9% carries the page number at the left margin (x 2.5%), a faint logo wordmark in the centre-left, and the source line right-aligned to about x 95.2%.

**Visuals**

One full-bleed photograph-quality reproduction of a colour illustration by Albert Robida, a late-19th-century print showing flying vehicles over Paris. Subject: a crowd of top-hatted and bustled figures in airborne carriages and boats. At left, a yellow streamlined airship-car with a cabin window and a small Eiffel Tower silhouette below it; a dark cloaked figure steers a red-brown craft in the centre; on the right a gentleman in a top hat and long dark frock coat stands on a yellow beaked, bird-like craft ornamented with two six-pointed star shapes, beside elegantly dressed women in pink and yellow feathered hats and gowns. Green Art Nouveau tendril shapes sit at lower right. Background sky is muted blue-grey with clouds; the lower-left shows a dense city of rooftops and small figures. Composition is a wide horizontal panorama, cropped at the top by the white header and at the bottom by the footer band; it is not boxed. Lighting is flat and even, as in a printed lithograph. Dominant colours: sage-teal #8FA3A6, mustard yellow #E0C84A, dusty pink #E8A0A0, muted green #8DBF7A, dark brown-black linework #2B2420. The footer band overlays the image with a white tint at 60% then 80% opacity, so the bottom of the illustration appears faded.

**Design**

Background: Solid white #FFFFFF. Clean white slide with no decorative shapes. Title and subtitle use Proxima Nova Regular, a geometric sans-serif, in normal weight and sentence case. Title is #101010 at about 5.9% of slide height, subtitle is #000000 at about 3.5%. Footer text is grey: page number #666666 and source #7F7E7B, both about 2.5%. No accent colours, lines, rules, corner radii or shadows. The footer band is white with 60% and 80% opacity layered over the image.

## Transcript

Captions (automatic) of the recording "Mobile Is Eating the World, 2016" (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3d7zZz_niTc). Timestamps are minutes and seconds into the recording.

[00:00] so I sometimes feel that talking about mobile now is a bit like talking about PCS in 2000 that this enormous thing has now happened and we're thinking about what we can do with it and so I think the first way to talk about this is to talk about deployment so if we look at a snapshot of the world today we're about halfway to connecting everybody there's about 5 and a half billion people on Earth over 14 there's a close to 5 billion people with a mobile phone about 2 and a half billion people have smartphones and that will go to 5 billion smartphones and so what's happening is that the mobile S curve is passing the PCS curve we have about one and a half billion PCS on Earth and you can see that growth coming up over the last couple of decades and now flattening out and starting to decline and then you can see how first mobile and now smartphones have accelerated past that heading towards pretty much the entire global population and so then if you sort of systematize that what tends to happen in technology is that each new technology follows an scurve and is then replaced by another one and in the early period of that S curve it

[01:03] begins as a crazy idea and then it becomes something that's growing very fast and there's a frenzy and there's Bubbles and then you move from the kind of the creation phase to the deployment phase where the questions are much more around scaling and maturity and then over time then there will be another rest curve that comes after that and mobile now is making that transition from creation to deployment and so what that means is that the issues that matter are changing so when we were in the creation phase and we talked about platform War and we talked about the latest handset and argued about technology and there were millions or tens or hundreds of millions of users and the key questions were sort of is this going to work and who's going to win and now we're in the deployment phase the winners are clear and we're in a phase of kind of steady incremental Improvement that each phone is a bit better than the one the one before a lot of the technology becomes commodity and we're at a phase where we have billions of users and so the question now is well what can we build with this thing what can we build with this amazing enormous thing that we built that everybody now has and so then to think about the things I'm going to talk about in this presentation I think there there's a couple of of elements to this firstly we

[02:05] have a new kind of scale for technology and we have new kinds of computing and those two things together give us new ways that we can change other Industries and I'm going to go on and then talk about a couple of those Industries first e-commerce and then cars so to talk about a new kind of scale first the obvious observation we've had a Changing of the Guard and the new guard are a lot bigger than the old guard so the new dominant companies in Tech are Google Apple Facebook and Amazon gaffa for short and they're about three times the scale of the old dominant companies in Tech which were winel that is to say Microsoft and Intel and so they're about three times the size in Revenue terms and that's pretty unsurprising but I think what's more interesting if you go on to the next slide is to think about how you compare the scale of the dominant companies today with the scale of Microsoft and Intel back in the 1990s when they were changing the world so we have this period of very rapid growth now how does that compare to the period of very rapid growth in the past and so back in the 9s winel grew their revenue 14 times and they looked amazing but if you compare that to the scale of Google Apple

[03:08] Facebook Amazon today they have 10 times the scale so they're much much bigger than Microsoft and Intel were back in the days when they were changing the world if you think in terms of valuation back in 1995 when Bill Gates was on every magazine cover on Earth Microsoft was actually not the biggest company on the stock exchange today Google Apple Facebook Amazon and yes Microsoft are all the five are the five biggest companies on the stock exchange so we've gone from being kind of important companies to being completely dominant companies to being much bigger much more powerful much more significant companies now that change in scale means much more investment annual capex across these companies has gone from $1 billion in 2000 to $34 billion in 2015 it also means many more people 10 times more people now over half a million people employed and what it really means I think fundamentally is that the tech Giants today have a completely different character of scale to IBM or witel before them it's not just that they're bigger it's that they're Giants in the economy rather than just Giants within the tech industry and also and I think this is also very important we have four of them and not just one so it's not IBM

[04:11] it's not Microsoft it's Google and apple and Facebook and Amazon all competing with each other and so you have things that flow out of that so apple is the 10th largest retailer on Earth by Revenue with over 53 billion of sales last year which is not bad for marketing operation if you then look at content and remember the days when content was King well for Amazon content is just a lever to get more Prime subscribers and sell more soap powder they don't even care about content as a primary method and Netflix of course is a Content company but Netflix itself has the fourth largest production budget in the USA so you have tech companies impinging a fundamental level on other Industries and there's a great quote from 2010 the chief executive Time Warner said that thinking of Netflix as a threat was like thinking that the Albanian Army was going to take over the world I suspect he probably wouldn't have that attitude today then to look at another sector again Intel the whole business of making chips has become an add-on for these companies so Google is making custom fpga chips for machine learning Amazon is making custom Asic chips for networking Apple makes the the system on a chip inside the iPhone it's the fastest Sac that there is on the market and it's making all sorts of other
